The unknown-account and locked-account paths derive\n * against {@link #dummyHash()}: the same memory-hard work against a constant credential that can\n * never match, so neither wall-clock time nor peak memory reveals which branch ran. There is no\n * sleep-based pad, so the padding cannot drift out of step with the configured cost. A successful\n * sign-in on an account whose stored parameters are below policy does one extra derivation to\n * upgrade it - a path that already required the correct password.\n *\n * <h2>Atomicity</h2>\n * One transaction per attempt, in a fixed order: consume the source budget, read the account, take\n * the {@code auth_guard} row lock, verify, then either clear or charge the account counter and\n * insert the session. Any failure rolls the whole attempt back, so a session cannot exist without\n * its account, and a failure cannot be charged to an account without the source budget also being\n * spent.\n *\n * <h2>Lockout is not a denial-of-access lever</h2>\n * The per-account counter is temporary ({@link Throttle#ACCOUNT_LOCK_SECONDS}) and self-clearing,\n * and a successful sign-in clears it, so knowing a colleague's email does not let an attacker keep\n * them out beyond one window. Total attempt rate is bounded by the per-source budget, which is\n * independent of the account: burning an account's five strikes costs the attacker five of their\n * own ten.\n */\npublic final class AuthService {\n\n    /** Single sign-in refusal for every failure mode, including a locked account. */\n    public static final String INVALID_MESSAGE = \"The email or password is not correct.\";\n\n    /** Shown when the audit trail or the store cannot be honoured: access is refused. */\n    public static final String INTERNAL_MESSAGE = \"Sign-in is unavailable right now. Try again later.\";\n\n    private final AccountStore accounts;\n    private final Throttle throttle;\n    private final SessionStore sessions;\n    private final PasswordHasher hasher;\n    private final Gate gate;\n    private final Clock clock;\n    private final String dummyHash;\n\n    /**\n     * Fixed internal buffer used to buy an equal-cost refusal. Argon2id's cost is set by its\n     * parameters, not by the length of the input, so a refusal can match the price of a real\n     * comparison without ever feeding an attacker-sized candidate into the KDF.\n     */\n    private static final char[] WORK_FILLER = \"equalising-derivation\".toCharArray();\n\n    public AuthService(AccountStore accounts, Throttle throttle, SessionStore sessions,\n        PasswordHasher hasher, Gate gate, Clock clock) {\n        this.accounts = accounts;\n        this.throttle = throttle;\n        this.sessions = sessions;\n        this.hasher = hasher;\n        this.gate = gate;\n        this.clock = clock;\n        // Derived once at construction: a genuine Argon2id string with a random salt whose\n        // password is random and kept nowhere, so nobody - including an operator with the\n        // database - can present it.\n        java.security.SecureRandom filler = new java.security.SecureRandom();\n        char[] throwaway = new char[24];\n        try {\n            for (int i = 0; i < throwaway.length; i++) {\n                throwaway[i] = (char) ('a' + filler.nextInt(26));\n            }\n            this.dummyHash = hasher.encode(throwaway);\n        } finally {\n            java.util.Arrays.fill(throwaway, '\\0');\n        }\n    }\n\n    public enum Status {\n        /** Credential accepted; a session was issued. */\n        AUTHENTICATED,\n        /** Generic refusal: malformed input, unknown account, or wrong password. */\n        INVALID_CREDENTIALS,\n        /** Refused by a rate limit; the client-visible message is identical to a bad password. */\n        THROTTLED,\n        /** Refused because a security control (audit trail or store) could not be honoured. */\n        UNAVAILABLE\n    }\n\n    public record Result(Status status, String message, SessionStore.Session session) {\n        public boolean authenticated() {\n            return status == Status.AUTHENTICATED;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * @param identifierChars email or username exactly as typed; never trimmed here\n     * @param passwordChars   candidate password; the caller erases it, this class never stores it\n     * @param sourceId        opaque caller-supplied source key (a digest in the CLI)\n     */\n    public Result login(char[] identifierChars, char[] passwordChars, String sourceId) {\n        InputPolicy.Identity identity;\n        try {\n            identity = InputPolicy.identifier(identifierChars);\n        } catch (InputPolicy.ValidationException invalidInput) {\n            return refuseWithoutStore(sourceId, \"identifier rejected\");\n        }\n        if (passwordChars == null || passwordChars.length == 0\n            || passwordChars.length > InputPolicy.PASSWORD_MAX) {\n            // Never enter the KDF with an attacker-sized buffer: the work factor would be theirs.\n            // The equalising derivation below uses this class's own fixed buffer instead.\n            return refuseWithoutStore(sourceId, \"password length rejected\");\n        }\n        try {\n            return attempt(identity, passwordChars, sourceId);\n        } catch (SQLException | AuditLog.AuditException storeOrAuditFault) {\n            // Fail closed: an unreadable store or an unwritable trail is a denial, not a bypass.\n            refuseQuietly(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_STORE_ERROR, sourceId, \"store or audit fault\");\n            return new Result(Status.UNAVAILABLE, INTERNAL_MESSAGE, null);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Refuses a sign-in whose input was rejected before any credential could be compared. The source\n     * budget is still charged, and a spent budget still returns the same generic line: otherwise\n     * sending malformed input would be free - an attacker could force a full key-derivation and an\n     * audit record on every attempt, forever, without ever touching the limit that is meant to bound\n     * online work. Charging here is what makes the per-source limit bound the expensive path, not only\n     * the well-formed one.\n     */\n    private Result refuseWithoutStore(String sourceId, String reason) {\n        try {\n            boolean allowed = Boolean.TRUE.equals(accounts.db().inTransaction(\n                connection -> throttle.tryConsumeSource(connection, Throttle.LOGIN_SOURCE_SCOPE, sourceId)));\n            if (!allowed) {\n                gate.record(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_THROTTLED_SOURCE, \"source\", gate.sourceTag(sourceId));\n                equaliseWork();\n                return throttled();\n            }\n            refuseQuietly(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_REFUSED_INVALID_INPUT, sourceId, reason);\n        } catch (SQLException | AuditLog.AuditException storeOrAuditFault) {\n            // The budget could not be consulted, so the attempt cannot be counted. Fail closed with\n            // the generic unavailable answer rather than serve a free derivation that escapes the limit.\n            refuseQuietly(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_STORE_ERROR, sourceId, \"store or audit fault on refusal\");\n            return new Result(Status.UNAVAILABLE, INTERNAL_MESSAGE, null);\n        }\n        equaliseWork();\n        return new Result(Status.INVALID_CREDENTIALS, INVALID_MESSAGE, null);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Buys one derivation so a refusal that never compared against the stored credential still\n     * costs what one that did. Without this, response time alone separates \"no such account\" and\n     * \"you are being rate limited\" from a wrong password, which is exactly the disclosure the\n     * uniform message was written to prevent.\n     */\n    private void equaliseWork() {\n        hasher.verify(WORK_FILLER.clone(), dummyHash);\n    }\n\n    private Result attempt(InputPolicy.Identity identity, char[] password, String sourceId)\n        throws SQLException, AuditLog.AuditException {\n        final Result[] outcome = new Result[1];\n        // Set by whichever branch performs a real comparison, so the caller can tell a refusal that\n        // already cost a derivation from one that cost none. The equalising derivation then runs\n        // <em>after</em> the transaction closes - never inside it - because the store monitor is\n        // held for the whole transaction and a padded derivation there would serialise every\n        // concurrent request behind it.\n        final boolean[] paidForWork = new boolean[1];\n        accounts.db().inTransaction(connection -> {\n            if (!throttle.tryConsumeSource(connection, Throttle.LOGIN_SOURCE_SCOPE, sourceId)) {\n                gate.record(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_THROTTLED_SOURCE, \"source\", gate.sourceTag(sourceId));\n                outcome[0] = throttled();\n                return Boolean.FALSE;\n            }\n\n            AccountStore.Account account = identity.byEmail()\n                ? accountBy(connection, \"email = ?\", identity.value())\n                : accountBy(connection, \"username = ?\", identity.value());\n\n            if (account == null) {\n                paidForWork[0] = true;\n                hasher.verify(password, dummyHash);\n                gate.record(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_FAILURE, \"reason\", \"no such account\",\n                    \"source\", gate.sourceTag(sourceId),\n                    \"identity\", identity.byEmail() ? gate.emailTag(identity.value())\n                        : gate.usernameTag(identity.value()));\n                outcome[0] = invalid();\n                return Boolean.FALSE;\n            }\n\n            Throttle.AccountGuard guard = throttle.lockAccountGuard(connection, account.id());\n            if (guard.isBlocked(clock.instant().getEpochSecond())) {\n                paidForWork[0] = true;\n                hasher.verify(password, dummyHash);\n                gate.record(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_THROTTLED_ACCOUNT, \"account\", gate.accountTag(account.id()),\n                    \"source\", gate.sourceTag(sourceId));\n                outcome[0] = throttled();\n                return Boolean.FALSE;\n            }\n\n            paidForWork[0] = true;\n            if (!hasher.verify(password, account.passwordHash())) {\n                long blockedUntil = throttle.chargeFailure(connection, account.id(), guard);\n                gate.record(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_FAILURE, \"reason\", \"credential mismatch\",\n                    \"account\", gate.accountTag(account.id()), \"source\", gate.sourceTag(sourceId),\n                    \"locked\", blockedUntil > 0 ? \"yes\" : \"no\");\n                if (blockedUntil > 0) {\n                    gate.record(AuditLog.Event.ACCOUNT_LOCKED, \"account\", gate.accountTag(account.id()),\n                        \"seconds\", Integer.toString(Throttle.ACCOUNT_LOCK_SECONDS));\n                }\n                outcome[0] = invalid();\n                return Boolean.FALSE;\n            }\n\n            throttle.clearFailures(connection, account.id());\n            if (guard.failures() > 0) {\n                // The counter was non-zero, so this sign-in ended a strike window: recording that\n                // lets an operator see a lockout was cleared by the real owner.\n                gate.record(AuditLog.Event.ACCOUNT_UNLOCKED, \"account\", gate.accountTag(account.id()),\n                    \"failures\", Integer.toString(guard.failures()));\n            }\n            if (hasher.weakerThanPolicy(account.passwordHash())) {\n                accounts.upgradePasswordHash(account.id(), password, account.passwordUpdatedAt());\n            }\n            SessionStore.Session session = sessions.issue(account.id());\n            gate.record(AuditLog.Event.LOGIN_SUCCESS, \"account\", gate.accountTag(account.id()),\n                \"source\", gate.sourceTag(sourceId), \"session\", gate.sessionTag(session.token()));\n            outcome[0] = new Result(Status.AUTHENTICATED, \"Sign-in complete.\", session);\n            return Boolean.TRUE;\n        });\n        if (outcome[0] == null) {\n            throw new SQLException(\"the sign-in transaction produced no outcome\");\n        }\n        if (!paidForWork[0]) {\n            // Reached by the rate-limited branch, which never read a credential. Pay the same\n            // price now, outside the store lock, so a throttle cannot be spotted by timing.\n            equaliseWork();\n        }\n        return outcome[0];\n    }\n\n    private static Result invalid() {\n        return new Result(Status.INVALID_CREDENTIALS, INVALID_MESSAGE, null);\n    }\n\n    private static Result throttled() {\n        // Deliberately the same wording as a wrong password: a locked account must not be\n        // distinguishable by the message either. The distinction is recorded in the audit trail.\n        return new Result(Status.THROTTLED, INVALID_MESSAGE, null);\n    }\n\n    private void refuseQuietly(AuditLog.Event event, String sourceId, String reason) {\n        try {\n            gate.record(event, \"reason\", reason, \"source\", gate.sourceTag(sourceId));\n        } catch (AuditLog.AuditException auditUnavailable) {\n            // The refusal has already happened and is the safe direction; this path never touched\n            // a credential, so there is nothing further to protect and no second channel to hide.\n            System.err.println(\"NOTE an audit record for a refused request could not be written\");\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static AccountStore.Account accountBy(Connection connection, String predicate, String value)\n        throws SQLException {\n        String sql = \"SELECT id, email, username, password_hash, kdf_memory_kib, kdf_iterations,\"\n            + \" kdf_parallelism, password_updated_at FROM accounts WHERE \" + predicate;\n        try (PreparedStatement query = connection.prepareStatement(sql)) {\n            query.setString(1, value);\n            try (ResultSet found = query.executeQuery()) {\n                if (!found.next()) {\n                    return null;\n                }\n                return new AccountStore.Account(found.getLong(1), found.getString(2), found.getString(3),\n                    found.getString(4), found.getInt(5), found.getInt(6), found.getInt(7), found.getLong(8));\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Validates a session token and resolves it to an account id, or {@code null}. */\n    public Long resolveSession(String token) {\n        try {\n            Long accountId = sessions.resolve(token);\n            if (accountId == null) {\n                gate.record(AuditLog.Event.SESSION_REJECTED, \"session\", gate.sessionTag(token));\n            }\n            return accountId;\n        } catch (SQLException | AuditLog.AuditException fault) {\n            return null;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Revokes the presented session. The result distinguishes \"a store/audit fault happened\" from\n     * the two client-indistinguishable outcomes, so the caller can fail closed on a fault while\n     * keeping a revoked session and a never-existing one worded identically.\n     */\n    public enum LogoutStatus {\n        /** A live session was ended by this call. */\n        REVOKED,\n        /** The token was well-formed but matched no live session; never disclosed as distinct. */\n        NO_LIVE_SESSION,\n        /** The store or trail could not be consulted: revocation is <em>not</em> confirmed. */\n        UNAVAILABLE\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Revokes the presented session. The client-visible outcome is kept uniform between\n     * {@link LogoutStatus#REVOKED} and {@link LogoutStatus#NO_LIVE_SESSION} by the caller, so this\n     * cannot probe session identifiers - but {@link LogoutStatus#UNAVAILABLE} is reported honestly:\n     * a bearer credential whose revocation the store could not confirm must not be reported as\n     * revoked, because that is the one case where a captured token would silently stay live.\n     * The record is written by {@link SessionStore#revoke}, the layer that actually ends the\n     * session, so no caller can end one without it appearing in the trail.\n     */\n    public LogoutStatus logout(String token) {\n        try {\n            return sessions.revoke(token) ? LogoutStatus.REVOKED : LogoutStatus.NO_LIVE_SESSION;\n        } catch (SQLException | AuditLog.AuditException fault) {\n            return LogoutStatus.UNAVAILABLE;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** The constant credential used to equalise work across failure branches. */\n    public String dummyHash() {\n        return dummyHash;\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"55b3fe55-9b33-4ceb-a98d-7284c35d4626","session_id":"35bb4370-4ba9-487b-baf7-07067cf38180","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"55b3fe55-9b33-4ceb-a98d-7284c35d4626","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"I have all the main source files read. Every method throws {@link AuditLog.AuditException} on a write failure, and callers\n * translate that into a refusal - the trail cannot be silently lost by an attacker who fills the\n * disk, replaces the log with a directory, or widens its permissions.\n *\n * <p>Field values passed through here are always code-supplied constants or non-reversible\n * digests; see {@link AuditLog}.\n */\npublic final class Gate {\n\n    /** Per-process salt so correlation tags do not survive as stable row identifiers. */\n    private final String processSalt = java.util.UUID.randomUUID().toString();\n\n    private final AuditLog audit;\n\n    public Gate(AuditLog audit) {\n        this.audit = audit;\n    }\n\n    /** Records the event or throws; there is no variant that swallows a failure. */\n    public void record(AuditLog.Event event, String... fields) throws AuditLog.AuditException {\n        audit.record(event, fields);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Non-reversible correlation tag for an account: the primary key hashed with a per-process\n     * random salt, so records can be joined without writing a row identifier that maps directly\n     * onto the database.\n     */\n    public String accountTag(long accountId) {\n        return tag(\"account\", Long.toString(accountId));\n    }\n\n    /** Non-reversible tag for a source identifier. */\n    public String sourceTag(String sourceId) {\n        if (sourceId == null) {\n            return \"unknown\";\n        }\n        return tag(\"src\", sourceId);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Tags for identifying data. Because a username or an email address is low-entropy, these are\n     * salted per process: records can be joined within one run of the program, but the trail cannot\n     * be joined back to database rows, and two runs of the program produce unrelated tags. See the\n     * README for exactly what that does and does not protect.\n     */\n    public String emailTag(String canonicalEmail) {\n        return tag(\"email\", canonicalEmail);\n    }\n\n    public String usernameTag(String username) {\n        return tag(\"username\", username);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Tag for a session token. A token is a bearer credential, so neither the token nor its\n     * unstored SHA-256 may appear in the trail: a trail reader could otherwise sign in with\n     * whatever they can read. This digest is additionally salted per process, so it cannot be\n     * matched against the {@code sessions} table either.\n     */\n    public String sessionTag(String token) {\n        if (token == null) {\n            return \"absent\";\n        }\n        return tag(\"session\", token);\n    }\n\n    private String tag(String kind, String value) {\n        return Digests.sha256Hex((processSalt + ':' + kind + ':' + value)\n            .getBytes(java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets.US_ASCII)).substring(0, 16);\n    }\n\n    public AuditLog log() {\n        return audit;\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"12014afc-31aa-440f-8071-37a50d21df3c","session_id":"35bb4370-4ba9-487b-baf7-07067cf38180","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"12014afc-31aa-440f-8071-37a50d21df3c","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Confirmed: the salt is per-process and explicitly documented as such (\"two runs of the program produce unrelated tags\"). On a real terminal with ICANON, the tty driver typically maps CR→NL (ICRNL), so you can't type a bare CR; so terminal-typed passwords can't contain CR in practice, and the piped path drops CR. The inconsistency claim: piped input silently deletes CR anywhere in the line. So `printf 'pa\\rss\\n' | app login` authenticates as if the password were `pass`. And `register` piped the same way stores hash of `pass` — consistent. So the real issue: (1) a CR in a piped credential is silently dropped rather than refused → the credential the user intended is not the credential verified (silent mutation, could cause a *wrong* account to be created with an unintended password, or an interactive-registration/ scripted-login mismatch); (2) it also means CR acts as nothing at all, while CR is a line terminator on classic Mac / in some generators — an attacker-crafted input file could make one line be read as a different credential than what a viewer shows.\n\nSeverity Low is right. I'll call it Medium with clear reasoning.\n\nB. SEC-cr-insession/password-mutation (readCapped `\\r` deletion): This is a genuine correctness/security bug: a password containing a CR byte (0x0D) in the middle, entered via a pipe (scripted/CI/automation, or a `printf` provisioning flow), has every CR byte silently deleted → the credential that gets registered/verified differs from the bytes supplied. Impact: (i) registration stores a hash of a mutated password — user thinks their password is X but it's X-without-CRs → silent credential corruption; (ii) two distinct passwords become equivalent (any password differing only in CR placement hashes the same) — credential collision/weakening; (iii) interop with the console path: `console.readPassword()` does NOT strip CRs, so the same password yields different values on TTY vs pipe → login failures, account lockout via self-inflicted lockout. Note the code strips CR only in the piped path; the TTY path keeps them. Severity: Medium. But it's the kind of silent authentication mutation that's arguably the most interesting genuine bug. Also note InputPolicy.password() rejects leading/trailing whitespace and control chars! Let me check: does it reject control characters inside the password? Let me look: password() checks length, leading/trailing whitespace... I saw lines 139-152 partial: after whitespace check, does it check ISOControl? On the TTY path, a CR would be kept by console.readPassword? Actually console.readPassword reads until newline and won't include CR. Hmm. So where does the CR removal actually change the effective password? If input has CR in middle piped: readCapped deletes it → password accepted without CR. User intended CR in password. So the mutation stands: the stored credential differs from intended. And it defeats InputPolicy's own stated defense (control chars must be rejected — the CR never reaches the check). SEC-toctou-password-file (Config.java:194-202): check-then-use on a path the caller controls via env var; `Files.isRegularFile(NOFOLLOW)` + mode check via `getPosixFilePermissions` (follows links!) then `Files.newInputStream(path)` (follows links). Exploit: requires an attacker to swap the file between check and open — a race window of microseconds — AND the app runs as the invoking user, so an attacker who can rename over the path already can read the file as that user... The realistic scenario: a setuid-ish/automation context where the db password file path is in a world-writable dir (e.g. /tmp/dbpw) — an attacker with write on the parent can swap in a symlink to another file; then the app opens the symlink target (following!) and the *first 1 MiB* of it becomes the DB password. That doesn't leak to the attacker directly... but: `hasGroupOrOtherAccess(path)` — `getPosixFilePermissions` FOLLOWS symlinks, so the check examines the *target's* mode; if the target is 0600 and owned by the app user, the check passes, then newInputStream reads the target — which is the same file, so consistent. Where it bites: TOCTOU — attacker passes the checks on a legit 0600 regular file, then swaps it for a symlink to `/etc/shadow`-ish or another user's secret; the app then reads that file as its DB password. Does that leak? The DB password becomes the secret of another file; H2 only uses it for auth. Its reachable effect: for the *data dir* and `.mv.db` checks, `hasGroupOrOtherAccess`/`isWorldWritable` follow symlinks — but `rejectSymlinkComponents` runs first on path components, and the final component check for `.mv.db` uses ensurePrivateStoreFile which does its own symlink rejection (per my earlier read). So the `permissions()` NOFOLLOW gap is largely mitigated. I should report the TOCTOU honestly: the real finding is check-then-open on `Files.newInputStream` without NOFOLLOW — the validated inode is not the opened inode (TOCTOU, CWE-367), and the open follows links while the check refused links. Severity: Low (needs parent-dir write by a third party + race; and README's own threat model has single-owner data dir). SEC-token-on-stdout (Main.java:255) — CWE-532 (Insertion of Sensitive Information into Log File). The CLI prints a `Set-Cookie:` header containing a live 256-bit bearer token to stdout, which in any wrapper (CI log, shell redirect, script tee, systemd journal via logger) persists the credential in cleartext, unencrypted, often world-readable, for the token's 8-hour life. Mitigations present: it's the CLI's documented purpose (emit a cookie for a portal). Severity: Medium (the credential is meant to be delivered to the caller, but stdout is a log-ish sink; and the file store is at-rest unencrypted while this leaks to logs). I'll call Medium.\n\nF. SEC-no-at-rest-encryption (Config.java:148/70-71) — CWE-311 — Medium. Backup/copy/core-dump/root/forensic imaging exposes emails + crackable hashes. README claims privacy rests on file mode. Medium is right (Argon2id mitigates hash exposure; emails don't).\n\nG. SEC-CR-mutation → Medium (above).\n\nH. SEC-bytes-vs-chars (Main.java:514 vs InputPolicy.java:31) — CWE-20 — Low. Piped cap is `PASSWORD_MAX + 1` = 257 bytes; a 257-byte buffer accepts up to 257 bytes before refusing; UTF-8 multibyte decoding of ≤257 bytes yields ≤257 chars, and InputPolicy rejects >256 chars → a 257-byte ASCII input would be refused by readCapped? Let's trace: buffer.length = 257. Loop writes while length < 257; when length == 257 (i.e., the 258th byte) → refuses. So it accepts up to 257 bytes. Then decoded chars ≤ 257 → InputPolicy rejects if >256 chars. So 257 ASCII bytes → passes reader, rejected by InputPolicy. Fine — defense in depth holds. Where's the gap? (i) 257 multibyte bytes → fewer chars → could be ≤256 chars of, e.g., 2-byte UTF-8 → 257 bytes = 128 chars → fine. (ii) The mismatch means the *reader's* promise (\"reads up to max bytes\") differs from the policy's unit; and `max+1` allows one byte beyond the stated cap. Real consequence: an attacker-supplied 257-byte line gets through the reader into `InputPolicy` (fine, it's caught) → so no bypass; this is a defense-in-depth/consistency defect, and there is one reachable asymmetry: for MAX_FIELD_CHARS=4096, the reader allows 4096 bytes; InputPolicy rejects >4096 chars; multi-byte UTF-8 means a 4096-byte field can be only ≤4096 chars → never exceeds. So no overflow. Therefore this is Low: \"buffer is sized max+1 and the cap is measured in a different unit than the policy that consumes it\" — a latent defect that will silently allow an over-cap input if a future caller's max is char-based and byte-truncated... I must be honest: it's Low with \"no demonstrated bypass\". Also the +1 means one extra byte is retained momentarily. OK — Low. Given honesty constraints, maybe I should fold the `max+1` observation into the CR finding? No — different CWE. Keep as Low CWE-20.\n\nI. Also `--db-password-file` mistyped → falls back to env/default; `--audit-log` mistyped → audit goes to default path, so security events land somewhere the operator isn't watching → silent audit loss. That's decent: Medium? I'd say Low-Medium. I'll set Medium: silent option ignore defeats explicit security-relevant overrides (audit destination, data dir, password file). the sentinel `\"shared-local\"` at Main.java:444. So when `/proc/self/status` is unreadable, ALL callers share one bucket key — one global 10-attempts/300 s budget for login and one for registration. That's a serious availability finding on its own: a whole-host login outage for 5 minutes at a time, triggerable by anyone who can run the program (each attempt consumes a token; 10 tokens per 300 s, so an attacker can keep the bucket empty continuously → nobody else can log in). And even with a readable uid, in the shared-service-account deployment the same applies to all users of that uid.\n\nThis raises severity to **High** in my judgment? Let's weigh: it's an availability control that an *unauthenticated local actor* (anyone able to exec the CLI — which is the entire threat surface here) can use to deny authentication service to all other users of that identity, persistently and at trivial cost (10 execs per 5 min, each ~0.2-0.6 s of work). Availability of the auth service is a core security property. Also, each of those 10 attempts triggers an Argon2id derivation inside the monitor (for the well-formed ones), adding latency for others (SEC-01 ties in). The code acknowledges the tradeoff explicitly and calls shared \"the fail-closed direction\", but shared+small+no-actor-separation = self-DoS lever. I'll rate this **High** — the easiest total-service denial in the codebase, and the README's residual #1 understates it (it frames the risk as \"weaker identity attribution\", not \"one actor denies everyone\").\n\nHmm, but is the CLI actually a multi-user service? \"This deliverable has no network layer\" — it's a CLI each user runs locally against a shared file DB. If the DB is a file on local disk with 0600 owned by one user, then only ONE OS user can even open it! In that deployment, there's exactly one user → no cross-user DoS. But README mentions a portal (Set-Cookie, portal_session) → the intended deployment is a service that shells out to this CLI, or a shared data dir. If all invocations run as one service account (the only way multiple users can share the 0600 DB), then all users share one throttle identity → DoS lever confirmed, and also means per-source limiting provides essentially zero protection against a malicious user of the portal.\n\nSo the finding holds for the only deployment where the throttle matters (multi-user front-end calling the CLI), and in that same deployment the per-source limit is also useless as an attacker control (one bad actor consumes everyone's budget; conversely an attacker is limited to 10/300 s total for the whole tenant, which is a *protection* for them... no wait, the attacker shares the bucket, so they can't spray more than 10/300s either — the throttle still bounds the attack rate but at the cost of cross-user denial).\n\nThat's a nuanced, honest finding: Medium-High. FOR UPDATE`; 8 h hard expiry; `isWellFormedToken` rejects anything not 64 hex before touching the store.\n8. Audit fail-closed: `Gate` wraps every `AuditLog.record`, and an `AuditException` propagates as a denial; login/logout/register all refuse when the trail cannot be written. (Retention is reported as SEC-02.)\n10. Log-injection: `AuditLog.escape()` bounds values to 256 chars and escapes quotes/backslashes/newlines/CR/TAB, control bytes and 0x7f; the event vocabulary is a closed enum and field keys are code-supplied → no attacker-controlled newline can forge a record.\n11. Link handling on the audit path: `guardFile()` requires a non-symlink regular file at exactly 0600 and re-checks on every append; `append` opens with `NOFOLLOW_LINKS` + WRITE/APPEND and no CREATE; size read with NOFOLLOW (AuditLog.java:208). (Contrast Config.java:385, reported as SEC-10.)\n12. Data-directory guards: `ensurePrivateDirectory` (0700), `ensurePrivateStoreFile` pre-creates `accounts.mv.db` as 0600 and refuses symlink/non-regular/wrong-mode; `rejectSymlinkComponents` walks every path component (Config.java:370-380).\n13. Throttle integrity: the per-source bucket is consumed by a single atomic UPDATE whose WHERE clause refuses when the window has elapsed and tokens are 0 (Throttle.java:78-107), so two racing callers cannot both take the last token; the per-account guard is read `FOR UPDATE` (Throttle.java:153) and written with a version bump; a genuine sign-in clears the strike counter; `window_seconds` is read from the row, so a legacy row's window cannot be reinterpreted.\n14. Fail-closed on store faults: SQLException/AuditException during login and logout → UNAVAILABLE with the generic message; logout never claims revocation it could not confirm.\n15. Authorization: session→account resolution is required before `revoke-sessions`/`whoami` act; `revoke-sessions` revokes only the resolved account's sessions; the CLI refuses a session cookie on the command line (Main.java:364-367) so credentials don't appear in process listings.\n16. Argument/credential hygiene: passwords and identifiers are held in `char[]` and zeroed in `finally` (Main.java:256-259); `Secrets.toString()` is redacted and `close()` zeroes.\n17. Dependency integrity: `get-deps.sh` verifies each jar against a pinned SHA-256 in `deps.lock`, fetched over TLS-only curl (`--proto '=https' --tlsv1.2`), requires two independent distributors to agree, cross-checks the published SHA-1, requires a named entry in the jar, and requires a jar signature where one exists; `build.sh` compiles with `-Xlint:all -Werror`; the runtime classpath carries only `bin`, H2 and Bouncy Castle (spring-security-crypto and commons-logging are test-only).\n18. Deserialization: no untrusted bytes are deserialized anywhere — no `ObjectInputStream`, no JSON/YAML/XML parsing of external input, no dynamic class loading — so there is no deserialization attack surface (checked with `exploiting-insecure-deserialization`; nothing to report).\n19. Now write the report.\n\nOne more consideration for SEC-01's \"attacker can fill the audit trail\" claim: what's the per-record size? Each line ~150-200 bytes. 64 MiB / 190 ≈ 355k records. Hmm — that's a lot! Can an unauthenticated local caller realistically generate 355,000 records? Per-source budget is 10 attempts / 300 s. That's 10 records per 5 min per uid → 2,880/day per uid. To reach 355k would take ~123 days for one uid. That materially weakens the \"unauthenticated flooding\" claim!\n\nWait — but `REGISTRATION_ATTEMPTED` is written *before* the throttle check, so a throttled register attempt still writes 1 record AND the REGISTRATION_THROTTLED record = 2 records per attempt, and the throttle only bounds... hmm, no: `tryConsumeSource` returns false after the budget is spent, and register returns THROTTLED — but each call still writes ATTEMPTED + THROTTLED records. So after the first 10 attempts, every subsequent attempt writes 2 records for free (no DB cost beyond the failed... wait, `gate.record` writes to the file; and tryConsumeSource does an UPDATE inside the txn which rolls back? The txn returns FALSE → committed? Let me think: `inTransaction` commits on normal return; `return Boolean.FALSE` is a normal return → the UPDATE (which consumed nothing since budget exhausted... the UPDATE returns rows and sets nothing) is committed. So each throttled register attempt = 2 audit records + 2 DB round trips, no rate limit at all on the *number of attempts* beyond the caller's own CPU!\n\nSo the register path is *unbounded*: the source budget limits account creation but does NOT limit the number of invocations — every invocation writes 2 audit records. So an attacker can loop `register` as fast as the CPU allows: each iteration = 2 audit records (~300 bytes) + a couple of SQL statements, no derivation (throttle check happens before encode). At, say, 500 iterations/sec single-threaded, that's 150 KB/s → 64 MiB in ~1.2 hours. Multi-process (bounded by the store monitor) — say 4-8 processes → tens of minutes. So YES: the trail can be wiped in about an hour by a busy unauthenticated local loop. And no throttle stops it (the budget is already spent, so no further cost, and each call still writes records). That's a genuinely strong High finding, and the \"audit control destroys the evidence\" mechanism is real.\n\nAlso each attempt does DB work → the loop is also a mild DoS, but the audit wipe is the sharp edge.
